Chemical Thermodynamics — Enthalpy, Entropy, Gibbs Free Energy & Hess's Law

Deep dive into Chemical Thermodynamics — enthalpy H = U + PV: exothermic (ΔH < 0, heat released) and endothermic (ΔH > 0, heat absorbed) reactions, standard enthalpy of formation ΔH_f° (the enthalpy change when one mole of a compound is formed from its elements in their standard states), bond dissociation energies (the enthalpy required to break a specific bond averaged over similar compounds, used to estimate reaction enthalpies: ΔH_R° = Σ BDE_broken − Σ BDE_formed). Entropy S (a measure of disorder or the number of microstates W: S = k_B ln W), the second law of thermodynamics (ΔS_universe > 0 for a spontaneous process), the third law (the entropy of a perfect pure crystal at 0 K is zero), standard molar entropies S° (absolute, not relative to formation). Gibbs free energy G = H − TS: ΔG = ΔH − TΔS, sign of ΔG determines spontaneity (ΔG < 0 spontaneous, ΔG = 0 at equilibrium, ΔG > 0 non-spontaneous), temperature dependence of spontaneity (ΔH and ΔS signs determine if a reaction is spontaneous at all, high, low, or no temperatures), the Gibbs-Helmholtz equation (ΔG = ΔH + T(∂ΔG/∂T)_P). Phase transitions: ΔG = 0 at equilibrium (melting point, boiling point), the Clausius-Clapeyron equation (ln P = −ΔH_vap/RT + C). Hess's law: ΔH for a reaction is the sum of ΔH for each step, regardless of the path (since enthalpy is a state function), allowing calculation of reaction enthalpies from known formation enthalpies: ΔH_R° = Σ ν ΔH_f°(products) − Σ ν ΔH_f°(reactants). Applications: formation enthalpies from combustion data, bond energy calculations, Born-Haber cycles for ionic compounds.

"The law that entropy always increases holds the supreme position among the laws of Nature."
— Arthur Eddington
Part 1

🔥 Enthalpy and Calorimetry

Enthalpy
Heat of Reaction, Standard Enthalpy of Formation, Bond Energies, and Calorimetry
Enthalpy H = U + PV is a state function whose change ΔH equals the heat transferred at constant pressure (q_P). For an exothermic reaction (ΔH < 0), the system releases heat to the surroundings; for endothermic (ΔH > 0), the system absorbs heat. The standard enthalpy of formation ΔH_f° is the enthalpy change when one mole of a compound is formed from its constituent elements in their standard states (1 bar, specified temperature, usually 298 K). By definition, ΔH_f° = 0 for elements in their standard states (e.g., O₂(g), C(graphite), Br₂(l)). Bond dissociation energy (BDE) is the enthalpy required to break a specific bond, averaged over similar molecules. For example, the O-H bond energy in water is about 463 kJ/mol. Reaction enthalpies can be estimated: ΔH_R° ≈ Σ BDE(reactants) − Σ BDE(products) — breaking bonds costs energy (endothermic), forming bonds releases energy (exothermic). Calorimetry measures heat changes: q = m c ΔT, where m is mass, c is specific heat capacity. A bomb calorimeter (constant volume) measures ΔU = q_V; a coffee-cup calorimeter (constant pressure) measures ΔH = q_P. The heat capacity at constant pressure C_P = (∂H/∂T)_P; at constant volume C_V = (∂U/∂T)_V. For ideal gases, C_P − C_V = nR. Standard enthalpy of combustion ΔH_comb° is the enthalpy change when one mole of a substance is completely burned in excess oxygen. Standard enthalpies of formation can be derived from combustion enthalpies using Hess's law. The Kirchhoff equation relates enthalpy change at different temperatures: ΔH(T₂) = ΔH(T₁) + ∫_{T₁}^{T₂} ΔC_P dT.
Enthalpy is the heat transferred at constant pressure; standard enthalpies of formation and bond energies serve as building blocks to calculate reaction enthalpies via Hess's law.
Part 2

🌀 Entropy and the Second Law

Entropy
Microstates, S = k ln W, the Second Law, and Standard Molar Entropies
Entropy S is a measure of the dispersal of energy or matter. The statistical definition, due to Boltzmann: S = k_B ln W, where W is the number of microstates (ways of arranging the energy and particles consistent with the macroscopic state). A spontaneous process is one that increases the total entropy of the universe: ΔS_univ = ΔS_sys + ΔS_surr > 0 (the second law of thermodynamics). For a reversible process at constant temperature, ΔS_sys = q_rev/T. For an irreversible process, ΔS_sys > q/T. The third law of thermodynamics: the entropy of a perfect crystal at absolute zero is zero (S(0 K) = 0), providing an absolute reference for entropy. Standard molar entropy S° (units J/mol·K) is the entropy of one mole of a substance in its standard state at 298 K. Unlike enthalpy, entropy has an absolute value (not a relative formation value). Gases have higher entropy than liquids, which have higher entropy than solids (gas > liquid > solid). Entropy increases with temperature, with molecular complexity, and with the number of moles of gas produced in a reaction. For a phase transition, ΔS_trans = ΔH_trans / T_trans. For example, ΔS_vap = ΔH_vap / T_b. The entropy change for a reaction: ΔS_R° = Σ ν S°(products) − Σ ν S°(reactants). The surroundings' entropy change: ΔS_surr = −ΔH_sys / T (for constant pressure). Thus the spontaneity condition ΔS_univ > 0 becomes ΔS_sys − ΔH_sys/T > 0, or ΔH_sys − T ΔS_sys < 0 — which is just −ΔG > 0.
Entropy quantifies the number of microscopic arrangements of a system; the second law dictates that the total entropy of the universe always increases for spontaneous processes.
Part 3

⚖️ Gibbs Free Energy and Spontaneity

Free Energy
ΔG = ΔH − TΔS, the Criterion for Spontaneity, and the Gibbs-Helmholtz Equation
The Gibbs free energy G = H − TS is a state function that combines enthalpy and entropy into a single criterion for spontaneity at constant temperature and pressure: a process is spontaneous when ΔG < 0, at equilibrium when ΔG = 0, and non-spontaneous when ΔG > 0. The defining equation at constant T: ΔG = ΔH − TΔS. The sign of ΔG depends on the signs and magnitudes of ΔH and ΔS. There are four cases: (1) ΔH < 0, ΔS > 0: spontaneous at all temperatures (e.g., 2H₂O₂ → 2H₂O + O₂). (2) ΔH > 0, ΔS < 0: non-spontaneous at all temperatures (the reverse is spontaneous). (3) ΔH < 0, ΔS < 0: spontaneous at low T, non-spontaneous at high T (the entropy cost dominates at high T). (4) ΔH > 0, ΔS > 0: spontaneous at high T, non-spontaneous at low T (entropy-driven at high T, e.g., melting of ice above 0°C). The equilibrium temperature for a phase transition is T = ΔH/ΔS (where ΔG = 0). The standard Gibbs free energy of formation ΔG_f° is analogous to ΔH_f°; ΔG_f° = 0 for elements. For a reaction: ΔG_R° = Σ ν ΔG_f°(products) − Σ ν ΔG_f°(reactants). The Gibbs-Helmholtz equation relates ΔG to temperature: ΔG = ΔH + T(∂ΔG/∂T)_P, or equivalently (∂(ΔG/T)/∂T)_P = −ΔH/T². This equation allows computing ΔG at different temperatures from ΔH at a reference temperature. The van't Hoff equation (d ln K/dT = ΔH°/RT²) connects equilibrium constants with temperature and will be explored in the Equilibrium lesson. The chemical potential μ_i = (∂G/∂n_i)_{T,P,n_j} is the partial molar Gibbs free energy: at equilibrium, the chemical potential of each component is equal in all phases.
Gibbs free energy unifies enthalpy and entropy into a single criterion for spontaneity (ΔG < 0); the balance between energetic (ΔH) and entropic (TΔS) contributions determines the temperature dependence.
Part 4

🧩 Hess's Law and State Functions

Hess's Law
Hess's Law, Thermochemical Cycles, and Applications to Formation and Combustion
Hess's law states that the enthalpy change of a reaction is independent of the path taken, depending only on the initial and final states — because enthalpy is a state function. This allows calculating ΔH for reactions that are difficult or impossible to measure directly. The general formula: ΔH_R° = Σ ν ΔH_f°(products) − Σ ν ΔH_f°(reactants), where ν are the stoichiometric coefficients. For example, to find ΔH for C(s) + ½O₂(g) → CO(g) (which cannot be measured directly because some CO₂ always forms), one uses: (1) C(s) + O₂(g) → CO₂(g) ΔH = −393.5 kJ, and (2) CO(g) + ½O₂(g) → CO₂(g) ΔH = −283.0 kJ. Reversing (2) and adding to (1): ΔH = −393.5 + 283.0 = −110.5 kJ. This is Hess's law in action. Thermochemical cycles include the Born-Haber cycle (for lattice energy of ionic compounds), the combustion cycle (deriving ΔH_f° from ΔH_comb° and known ΔH_f° of CO₂ and H₂O), and the enthalpy of reaction from bond energies. Enthalpy of formation from combustion data: for a hydrocarbon C_xH_y, ΔH_f° = x ΔH_f°(CO₂) + (y/2) ΔH_f°(H₂O) − ΔH_comb°. Enthalpy of reaction from bond energies: ΔH_R° = Σ BDE(reactants) − Σ BDE(products) — this is an approximation (bond energies are averages over many compounds) but useful for estimating enthalpies when experimental data are unavailable. The same principle extends to other state functions: ΔS_R° = Σ ν S°(products) − Σ ν S°(reactants), and ΔG_R° = Σ ν ΔG_f°(products) − Σ ν ΔG_f°(reactants). The key insight: because U, H, S, and G are all state functions, their changes depend only on initial and final states, making thermochemical cycles possible.
Hess's law, a direct consequence of enthalpy being a state function, allows the calculation of reaction enthalpies via algebraic combination of known thermochemical equations — the foundation of thermochemistry.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Chemistry

Temperature Dependence of Reaction Enthalpy and Kirchhoff’s Law

The standard enthalpy change of a reaction ΔH° is tabulated at 298 K, yet most practical processes occur at other temperatures. Kirchhoff’s law provides the rigorous link: the temperature derivative of ΔH at constant pressure equals the difference in heat capacities between products and reactants.

Mathematical statement: (∂ΔH/∂T)P = ΔCp = Σν Cp(products) − Σν Cp(reactants). Integrating from a reference temperature T₁ (usually 298 K) to a target temperature T₂ gives ΔH(T₂) = ΔH(T₁) + ∫T₁T₂ ΔCp dT.

Heat‑capacity models. For gases, Cp is often expressed as a polynomial in temperature: Cp = a + bT + cT² + dT⁻² (Shomate equation) or the simpler NASA 7‑coefficient form. For condensed phases, Cp is frequently treated as constant over modest ranges, but high‑precision work uses the same polynomial approach.

Practical integration. When ΔCp is approximated as constant, ΔH(T₂) ≈ ΔH(T₁) + ΔCp(T₂ − T₁). If polynomial coefficients are available, the integral is evaluated term‑by‑term: ∫(a + bT + cT² + dT⁻²)dT = aΔT + ½b(T₂²−T₁²) + ⅓c(T₂³−T₁³) − d(1/T₂ − 1/T₁).

Example. For the synthesis of ammonia, N₂(g) + 3 H₂(g) → 2 NH₃(g), standard enthalpies of formation at 298 K give ΔH°₍₂₉₈₎ = −92.2 kJ mol⁻¹. Using Shomate coefficients for each species, ΔCp at 500 K evaluates to about −45 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹. The integrated correction from 298 K to 500 K is roughly −9 kJ mol⁻¹, yielding ΔH°₍₅₀₀₎ ≈ −101 kJ mol⁻¹. This temperature correction is essential for accurate equilibrium‑constant predictions via the van’t Hoff equation.

Limitations. Kirchhoff’s law assumes no phase change occurs between T₁ and T₂. If a reactant or product melts, boils, or undergoes a solid‑solid transition, the enthalpy of that transition must be added explicitly, and the heat‑capacity integration must be split at the transition temperature.

Maxwell Relations and Thermodynamic Potentials

Four fundamental thermodynamic potentials — internal energy U, enthalpy H, Helmholtz free energy A, and Gibbs free energy G — are linked by Legendre transforms. Each potential is a natural function of a specific pair of variables, and the exactness of its total differential yields the Maxwell relations, which connect measurable derivatives.

Definitions and natural variables

  • U(S, V): dU = T dS − P dV
  • H(S, P): dH = T dS + V dP
  • A(T, V): dA = −S dT − P dV
  • G(T, P): dG = −S dT + V dP

Because each differential is exact, the mixed second derivatives are equal. Equating ∂²U/∂V∂S = ∂²U/∂S∂V gives (∂T/∂V)S = −(∂P/∂S)V. Repeating for the other potentials produces the four classic Maxwell relations:

Maxwell Relations
PotentialRelation
U(S,V)(∂T/∂V)S = −(∂P/∂S)V
H(S,P)(∂T/∂P)S = (∂V/∂S)P
A(T,V)(∂S/∂V)T = (∂P/∂T)V
G(T,P)(∂S/∂P)T = −(∂V/∂T)P

Utility. These relations replace difficult entropy derivatives with readily measurable volume, pressure, and temperature derivatives. For instance, (∂S/∂P)T = −(∂V/∂T)P = −Vα, where α is the coefficient of thermal expansion. This allows calculation of entropy changes during isothermal compression from PVT data alone.

Deriving thermodynamic identities

Combining Maxwell relations with definitions of heat capacities yields powerful identities. The difference between CP and CV is CP − CV = TVα²/κT, where κT = −(1/V)(∂V/∂P)T is the isothermal compressibility. The Joule‑Thomson coefficient μJT = (∂T/∂P)H = (Tα − 1)/CP V follows from the H‑based Maxwell relation and the definition of enthalpy.

Application to real gases

For a van der Waals gas, (∂P/∂T)V = R/(V−b). Using the A‑based Maxwell relation, (∂S/∂V)T = R/(V−b). Integration gives the entropy change on isothermal expansion: ΔS = R ln[(V₂−b)/(V₁−b)], which reduces to the ideal‑gas result when b → 0. Such derivations illustrate how Maxwell relations bridge microscopic equations of state and macroscopic thermodynamic quantities.

Worked Example: Using a Maxwell Relation
For one mole of a van der Waals gas (a = 1.39 Pa m⁶ mol⁻², b = 3.91×10⁻⁵ m³ mol⁻¹) at 300 K, calculate the entropy change when the volume increases from 0.02 m³ to 0.05 m³ isothermally.
From the Maxwell relation derived from Helmholtz free energy: (∂S/∂V)T = (∂P/∂T)V. For a van der Waals gas, P = RT/(V−b) − a/V², so (∂P/∂T)V = R/(V−b). Integrate: ΔS = ∫V₁V₂ R/(V−b) dV = R ln[(V₂−b)/(V₁−b)]. Insert values: R = 8.314 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹, V₁ = 0.02 m³, V₂ = 0.05 m³, b = 3.91×10⁻⁵ m³. ΔS = 8.314 ln[(0.05−3.91×10⁻⁵)/(0.02−3.91×10⁻⁵)] ≈ 8.314 ln(2.499) ≈ 8.314 × 0.916 ≈ 7.62 J K⁻¹. The entropy increase is 7.6 J K⁻¹.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a derivative like (∂S/∂P)T or (∂V/∂T)P, immediately write the corresponding Maxwell relation from the G‑potential. Identify the measurable quantity (α, κT, or an equation‑of‑state derivative) and substitute. This avoids memorising dozens of individual formulas and works for any simple compressible system.

Chemical Potential, Activity, and Non‑Ideal Systems

The chemical potential μi = (∂G/∂ni)T,P,nj≠i is the partial molar Gibbs free energy. For a pure ideal gas, μ = μ° + RT ln(P/P°). In real systems the effective pressure or concentration is replaced by the activity ai, defined so that μi = μi° + RT ln ai retains the same formal structure.

Fugacity and gas‑phase activity

For a real gas, the activity is the fugacity f divided by the standard pressure P°: a = f/P°. Fugacity is defined by the requirement that the chemical potential expression remains valid: μ = μ° + RT ln(f/P°). The fugacity coefficient φ = f/P quantifies deviation from ideality; φ → 1 as P → 0. It can be obtained from the compressibility factor Z = PV/RT via ln φ = ∫₀P (Z−1)/P dP.

Solution‑phase activity

In liquid solutions the activity of solute i is ai = γi xi (mole‑fraction basis) or ai = γi mi/m° (molality basis), where γi is the activity coefficient. For the solvent, the Raoult’s‑law standard state gives asolvent = γsolvent xsolvent with γ → 1 as x → 1. For the solute, the Henry’s‑law standard state gives asolute = γsolute xsolute with γ → 1 as x → 0.

Debye–Hückel limiting law

For dilute electrolyte solutions, the mean ionic activity coefficient γ± follows log₁₀ γ± = −A |z₊z₋| √I, where I = ½ Σ mizi² is the ionic strength, A ≈ 0.509 mol⁻¹ᐟ² kg¹ᐟ² at 298 K in water, and z are ionic charges. The extended Debye–Hückel equation adds a linear term in I to improve accuracy up to I ≈ 0.1 mol kg⁻¹.

Reaction quotient in terms of activities

The thermodynamic equilibrium constant K is defined by the product of activities raised to stoichiometric powers: K = Π aiνi. The reaction quotient Q uses the same expression with instantaneous activities. The Gibbs energy of reaction at any composition is ΔG = ΔG° + RT ln Q, and at equilibrium ΔG = 0 gives ΔG° = −RT ln K. This formulation is exact; all non‑ideality is absorbed into the activity coefficients.

Phase Equilibria and the Clapeyron Equation

At equilibrium between two phases α and β of a pure substance, the chemical potentials are equal: μα(T,P) = μβ(T,P). Differentiating along the coexistence curve gives the Clapeyron equation: dP/dT = ΔS/ΔV = ΔH/(T ΔV), where ΔS, ΔV, ΔH are the molar entropy, volume, and enthalpy changes for the transition α → β.

Solid–liquid equilibrium

For melting, ΔV is usually positive (water is a notable exception). Since ΔHfus > 0, the slope dP/dT is positive for most substances. The melting curve is nearly linear over moderate ranges because ΔH and ΔV vary slowly. For water, ΔV < 0 gives a negative slope, explaining why ice melts under pressure.

Liquid–vapor and solid–vapor equilibrium

Here ΔV ≈ Vgas (since Vcondensed ≪ Vgas). Using the ideal‑gas approximation Vgas = RT/P, the Clapeyron equation becomes the Clausius–Clapeyron equation: d(ln P)/dT = ΔHvap/(RT²). Integration assuming constant ΔH yields ln(P₂/P₁) = −ΔHvap/R (1/T₂ − 1/T₁). This relation underpins the estimation of boiling points at different pressures and the determination of ΔHvap from vapor‑pressure data.

Triple point and critical point

The triple point is the unique (T,P) where solid, liquid, and vapor coexist; it satisfies the three Clapeyron equations simultaneously. The critical point terminates the liquid–vapor coexistence curve; there ΔV → 0 and ΔH → 0, so dP/dT remains finite. Near the critical point, the Clapeyron equation fails because the phases become indistinguishable and fluctuations dominate.

Application to polymorphism

For solid–solid transitions (e.g., graphite diamond), ΔV is small but non‑zero. The Clapeyron slope determines the pressure required for synthesis at a given temperature. For the graphite‑diamond transition, ΔV ≈ −1.9 cm³ mol⁻¹ and ΔH ≈ 1.9 kJ mol⁻¹ at 298 K, giving dP/dT ≈ 3.5 MPa K⁻¹. This steep positive slope explains why high pressure (≈ 1.5 GPa) and high temperature (≈ 1800 K) are needed for diamond formation.

Electrochemical Thermodynamics: ΔG, Cell Potential, and the Nernst Equation

Electrochemical cells convert chemical free energy into electrical work. The maximum non‑expansion work obtainable from a galvanic cell at constant T and P equals the decrease in Gibbs free energy: ΔG = −n F E, where n is the number of electrons transferred per reaction event, F = 96 485 C mol⁻¹ is Faraday’s constant, and E is the cell potential.

Standard potentials and the equilibrium constant

For a cell reaction written as a reduction, the standard cell potential E° relates to the standard Gibbs energy: ΔG° = −n F E°. Combining with ΔG° = −RT ln K gives the fundamental link E° = (RT/nF) ln K. At 298 K, (RT/F) ln 10 ≈ 0.05916 V, so log₁₀ K = n E°/0.05916. This allows calculation of equilibrium constants from tabulated standard reduction potentials.

The Nernst equation

For non‑standard conditions, the cell potential depends on the reaction quotient Q expressed in activities: E = E° − (RT/nF) ln Q. For a half‑reaction aA + ne⁻ ⇌ bB, the reduction potential is E = E° − (RT/nF) ln(aBb/aAa). In dilute aqueous solutions, activities are approximated by concentrations (or partial pressures for gases), yielding the familiar form E = E° − (0.05916/n) log₁₀ Q at 298 K.

Temperature dependence of cell potential

Differentiating ΔG = −nFE with respect to T at constant P gives (∂E/∂T)P = ΔS/(nF). The entropy change of the cell reaction can therefore be obtained from the temperature coefficient of the emf. Conversely, ΔH = ΔG + TΔS = −nF(E − T(∂E/∂T)P). These relations are the basis of thermodynamic analysis of batteries and fuel cells.

Concentration cells and liquid‑junction potentials

A concentration cell has identical electrodes but different electrolyte activities. Its emf arises solely from the activity difference: E = (RT/nF) ln(a₂/a₁). In practice, a liquid‑junction potential develops at the interface between solutions of different composition; the measured emf includes this contribution unless a salt bridge with a non‑selective electrolyte (e.g., KCl) minimizes it.

Applications

Standard potentials are used to construct the electrochemical series, predicting spontaneity of redox reactions. The Nernst equation underpins pH measurement (glass electrode), corrosion potential calculations, and the design of electrochemical sensors. In energy storage, the open‑circuit voltage of a lithium‑ion cell follows the Nernst equation with the activity of Li⁺ in the host materials.

  • Assuming ΔCp = 0 over large temperature intervals when Kirchhoff’s law requires integration of temperature‑dependent heat capacities.
  • Confusing the Maxwell relation derived from G (∂S/∂P)T = −(∂V/∂T)P with the one from A (∂S/∂V)T = (∂P/∂T)V.
  • Using concentrations instead of activities in the Nernst equation for solutions beyond the dilute limit, ignoring activity‑coefficient corrections.
  • Applying the Clausius–Clapeyron equation to solid–liquid transitions where ΔV is not negligible compared with the gas volume.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of ΔH in the van’t Hoff equation when the reaction enthalpy varies significantly with temperature.
  • Treating fugacity coefficient φ as unity for gases at moderate pressures (e.g., > 10 bar) where real‑gas deviations are appreciable.
  • Forgetting that the standard state for a solute in the Henry’s‑law convention is the hypothetical 1 mol kg⁻¹ solution with γ = 1, not the pure solute.

Statistical Thermodynamics: Partition Functions and Macroscopic Properties

The bridge between microscopic quantum states and macroscopic thermodynamic functions is the partition function. For a system of N distinguishable, non‑interacting particles the canonical partition function is Q = qN/N! where q is the molecular partition function. The molecular partition function factorises into translational, rotational, vibrational and electronic contributions: q = qtrans qrot qvib qelec. Each factor is a sum over the corresponding energy levels: q = Σi gi exp(−εi/kT). From q one obtains the internal energy U = NkT2(∂ln q/∂T)V, the entropy S = Nk[ln q + T(∂ln q/∂T)V] + k ln(N!/NN), and the Helmholtz free energy A = −NkT ln(q/N) − kT ln N!. The Gibbs free energy follows from G = A + pV. For an ideal gas the translational partition function yields the Sackur–Tetrode equation for the molar entropy: Sm = R[ln((2πMkT/h2)3/2 kT/p) + 5/2]. Rotational partition functions for linear and non‑linear molecules are qrot = T/σθrot and qrot = √(π/σ) (T3/2/(θAθBθC)1/2) respectively, where σ is the symmetry number and θrot = h2/8π2Ik. Vibrational contributions use the harmonic oscillator approximation: qvib = Πj [1 − exp(−hνj/kT)]−1. The equilibrium constant K for a gas‑phase reaction aA + bB ⇌ cC + dD is expressed in terms of molecular partition functions per unit volume: K = (qC/V)c (qD/V)d / (qA/V)a (qB/V)b × exp(−Δε0/kT), where Δε0 is the zero‑point energy difference. This formulation shows explicitly how isotopic substitution changes K through mass‑dependent translational and rotational partition functions, a classic examination topic.

Thermodynamics of Surfaces: Gibbs Adsorption Isotherm and Surface Tension

Interfaces possess an excess free energy proportional to their area; the proportionality constant is the surface tension γ (J m−2 or N m−1). Gibbs introduced a dividing surface to separate bulk phases and defined the surface excess Γi of component i as the amount per unit area in excess of what would be present if the bulk concentrations extended uniformly to the dividing surface. For a two‑component system (solvent 1, solute 2) the Gibbs adsorption equation at constant temperature reads dγ = −Γ2 dμ2, where μ2 is the chemical potential of the solute. Using μ2 = μ2° + RT ln a2 and assuming dilute solution (a2 ≈ c2), the equation integrates to the Gibbs adsorption isotherm: Γ2 = −(1/RT)(dγ/d ln c2). A negative slope of γ versus ln c2 indicates positive adsorption (surfactants), while a positive slope indicates negative adsorption (inorganic electrolytes). The Langmuir adsorption model relates surface coverage θ = Γ/Γmax to bulk concentration: θ = Kc/(1+Kc), where K is the adsorption equilibrium constant. Combining with the Gibbs equation yields the Szyszkowski equation: γ = γ0 − RT Γmax ln(1+Kc). This predicts the limiting surface tension reduction at high surfactant concentration and the critical micelle concentration (CMC) where γ becomes nearly constant. Surface tension also governs capillary rise (h = 2γ cosθ/ρgr) and the Kelvin equation for curved liquid–vapor interfaces: ln(p/p0) = 2γVm/rRT. These relations are essential for understanding nucleation, aerosol stability, and the thermodynamics of emulsions.

Worked Example: Gibbs Adsorption Isotherm
The surface tension of an aqueous surfactant solution varies with concentration c (mol L−1) as γ = 72.8 − 12.5 ln(1 + 250c) mN m−1. Calculate the surface excess Γ at c = 0.001 mol L−1 and 298 K.
Differentiate γ with respect to ln c: dγ/d ln c = dγ/dc × c. First, dγ/dc = −12.5 × (250)/(1+250c) = −3125/(1+250c) mN m−1 L mol−1. At c = 0.001 M, 1+250c = 1.25, so dγ/dc = −2500 mN m−1 L mol−1. Then dγ/d ln c = −2500 × 0.001 = −2.5 mN m−1. Convert to SI: −2.5 × 10−3 N m−1. Gibbs equation: Γ = −(1/RT) dγ/d ln c. R = 8.314 J mol−1 K−1, T = 298 K, RT = 2478 J mol−1. Γ = −(−2.5×10−3)/2478 = 1.01×10−6 mol m−2. The positive value confirms surfactant adsorption.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem gives γ(c) in a logarithmic form, differentiate analytically before substituting numbers. Remember that dγ/d ln c = c dγ/dc. Keep units consistent: convert mN m−1 to N m−1 and concentration to mol m−3 if using SI R, or stay in L mol−1 and use R = 8.314×10−3 kJ mol−1 K−1 with γ in kN m−1. The sign of Γ is a quick check: surfactants give Γ > 0.

Non‑Equilibrium Thermodynamics: Entropy Production and Onsager Reciprocal Relations

Classical thermodynamics deals with equilibrium states; non‑equilibrium thermodynamics extends the framework to systems with continuous flows of matter, heat, or charge. The local entropy production rate σ (W K−1 m−3) is expressed as a sum of products of thermodynamic fluxes Jk and their conjugate forces Xk: σ = Σ Jk Xk ≥ 0. For heat conduction, Jq = −κ∇T and Xq = ∇(1/T), giving σ = κ(∇T)2/T2. For diffusion of species i, Ji = −Di∇ci and Xi = −∇(μi/T). In the linear regime near equilibrium, fluxes are linear functions of all forces: Ji = Σ Lik Xk. Onsager’s reciprocal relations state that the phenomenological coefficient matrix is symmetric: Lik = Lki, provided the forces and fluxes are chosen such that σ = Σ Ji Xi and microscopic reversibility holds. This symmetry couples otherwise independent processes, e.g., thermodiffusion (Soret effect) where a temperature gradient induces a mass flux, and the Dufour effect where a concentration gradient drives a heat flux. The Seebeck, Peltier, and Thomson effects in thermoelectricity are classic manifestations: Je = Lee Xe + Leq Xq, Jq = Lqe Xe + Lqq Xq with Leq = Lqe. The figure of merit ZT = S2σT/κ (S = Seebeck coefficient, σ = electrical conductivity, κ = thermal conductivity) quantifies thermoelectric efficiency. Onsager relations also underpin the derivation of the Nernst–Planck equation for ion transport in electrochemical systems when the electric field is treated as a thermodynamic force.

Binary Phase Diagrams: Lever Rule, Eutectic and Peritectic Systems

Binary phase diagrams map the equilibrium phases of two components as a function of temperature and composition at constant pressure. The Gibbs phase rule F = C − P + 1 (constant pressure) gives the degrees of freedom. In a single‑phase region (P = 1) F = 2, so both T and composition can vary independently. In a two‑phase region (P = 2) F = 1; fixing T determines the compositions of the coexisting phases (the liquidus and solidus curves). The lever rule quantifies the relative amounts of the two phases: for an overall composition x0 between phase compositions xα and xβ, the fraction of phase α is (xβ − x0)/(xβ − xα) and the fraction of phase β is (x0 − xα)/(xβ − xα). A eutectic system exhibits a liquidus curve that descends from the melting points of the pure components to a minimum at the eutectic composition xe and temperature Te. At the eutectic point three phases (liquid, α, β) coexist (P = 3, F = 0). Upon cooling a liquid of eutectic composition, it solidifies isothermally into a fine mixture of α and β (eutectic microstructure). Off‑eutectic compositions solidify over a temperature range, producing primary crystals of one phase followed by the eutectic reaction. A peritectic system shows a liquidus curve that rises from one component, meets a solidus curve, and at the peritectic temperature Tp a liquid of composition xL reacts with a solid phase α of composition xα to form a new solid phase β of composition xβ: L + α → β. The peritectic reaction is often sluggish, leading to cored microstructures. Phase diagrams for systems with intermediate compounds (e.g., Mg2Ni) treat the compound as a pseudo‑component, splitting the diagram into two simpler eutectic subsystems. The Clausius–Clapeyron relation applied to the liquidus curve gives dT/dx = RT2/ΔHfus (for ideal solutions), linking the slope to the enthalpy of fusion.

Transition State Theory: Eyring Equation and Thermodynamic Activation Parameters

Transition state theory (TST) provides a thermodynamic formulation of chemical kinetics. The reaction A + B ⇌ [AB]‡ → products assumes a quasi‑equilibrium between reactants and the activated complex [AB]‡. The rate constant k = κ (kBT/h) K‡, where κ is the transmission coefficient (often ≈ 1), kB is Boltzmann’s constant, h is Planck’s constant, and K‡ is the equilibrium constant for formation of the activated complex from reactants in their standard states. Expressing K‡ in terms of standard Gibbs energy of activation ΔG‡ = −RT ln K‡ yields the Eyring equation: k = κ (kBT/h) exp(−ΔG‡/RT). ΔG‡ = ΔH‡ − TΔS‡, where ΔH‡ and ΔS‡ are the standard enthalpy and entropy of activation. An Eyring plot of ln(k/T) versus 1/T gives a straight line with slope −ΔH‡/R and intercept ln(κkB/h) + ΔS‡/R. For a bimolecular gas‑phase reaction, the pre‑exponential factor A in the Arrhenius equation k = A exp(−Ea/RT) relates to ΔS‡ via A = (kBT/h) e1+ΔS‡/R (for standard state 1 bar). A large negative ΔS‡ indicates a highly ordered transition state (e.g., associative mechanisms), while a positive ΔS‡ suggests a dissociative or loose transition state. The kinetic isotope effect (KIE) arises from differences in zero‑point vibrational energies between isotopologues in the transition state versus reactants; TST predicts kH/kD ≈ exp[(ΔZPEreact − ΔZPE‡)/RT]. TST also underpins the Marcus theory of electron transfer, where the activation free energy ΔG‡ = (λ + ΔG°)2/4λ, with λ the reorganization energy.

  • Confusing the symmetry number σ in rotational partition functions with the reaction stoichiometric coefficient.
  • Using the Gibbs adsorption equation without converting concentration units to match the gas constant R (e.g., mixing mol L−1 with R in J mol−1 K−1).
  • Assuming Onsager reciprocity holds far from equilibrium; it is strictly valid only in the linear regime near equilibrium.
  • Applying the lever rule to three‑phase regions (eutectic or peritectic points) where the phase fractions are fixed by the reaction stoichiometry, not by the overall composition.
  • Neglecting the transmission coefficient κ in the Eyring equation when comparing experimental pre‑exponential factors with theoretical ΔS‡ values.
  • Treating the standard state for ΔG‡ as 1 M for gas‑phase reactions; the correct standard state is 1 bar, which introduces an RT ln(RT/p°) correction.
  • Forgetting that the Clausius–Clapeyron slope for a liquidus curve in a binary diagram uses the partial molar enthalpy of fusion of the component, not the total enthalpy of fusion of the pure substance.
The advanced sections above complete the thermodynamic toolkit needed for rigorous analysis of equilibrium, interfacial, non‑equilibrium, phase‑diagram, and kinetic phenomena.

Thermodynamic Stability, Metastability, and Nucleation Theory

The curvature of the Gibbs free‑energy surface with respect to composition or order parameter determines whether a homogeneous phase is stable, metastable, or unstable. For a binary mixture at constant temperature and pressure the molar Gibbs energy G(x) is convex in the stable region (∂²G/∂x² > 0). The binodal curve marks the limit of metastability where the common‑tangent construction yields two coexisting compositions of equal chemical potential. Inside the binodal but outside the spinodal (∂²G/∂x² < 0) the system is metastable: a finite‑amplitude fluctuation is required to overcome the nucleation barrier. The spinodal defines the limit of absolute instability; any infinitesimal composition wave grows spontaneously, leading to spinodal decomposition.

Classical nucleation theory (CNT) treats the formation of a spherical nucleus of the new phase as a competition between a bulk free‑energy gain (−ΔGv V) and a surface penalty (γ A). For a spherical nucleus of radius r, ΔG(r) = −(4π/3)ΔGvr³ + 4πγr². The critical radius r* = 2γ/ΔGv corresponds to the maximum of ΔG; the nucleation barrier ΔG* = (16π/3)γ³/ΔGv². The steady‑state homogeneous nucleation rate J = J₀ exp(−ΔG*/kBT) where J₀ contains a kinetic prefactor (Zeldovich factor, attachment frequency). Heterogeneous nucleation on a substrate reduces the barrier by a geometric factor f(θ) = (2 + cos θ)(1 − cos θ)²/4, where θ is the contact angle; even a modest wetting angle can lower ΔG* by orders of magnitude.

Experimental verification comes from supersaturated vapours, supercooled liquids, and polymer blends. In vapour condensation the Kelvin equation links the equilibrium vapour pressure over a curved interface to the radius: ln(p/p₀) = 2γVm/rRT. In polymer solutions the Flory–Huggins χ parameter controls the depth of the free‑energy well and thus the nucleation barrier. Metastable lifetimes can span seconds to geological times; the Avrami equation describes the overall transformation kinetics when nucleation and growth occur simultaneously. Understanding the distinction between spinodal decomposition (continuous composition modulation) and nucleation‑and‑growth (discrete droplets) is essential for interpreting microstructures in alloys, protein crystallization, and atmospheric aerosol formation.

Flory–Huggins Theory of Polymer Solutions

The Flory–Huggins lattice model treats a polymer solution as a regular solution on a lattice of N sites, each occupied by either a solvent molecule (size = 1 segment) or a polymer segment. A polymer chain of degree of polymerization r occupies r contiguous sites. The combinatorial entropy of mixing per lattice site is ΔSmix/kB = −[ϕ1 ln ϕ1 + (ϕ2/r) ln ϕ2], where ϕ1 and ϕ2 are the volume fractions of solvent and polymer. The enthalpy of mixing per site is ΔHmix/kB = χ ϕ1ϕ2, with χ the Flory–Huggins interaction parameter (often expressed as χ = A + B/T). The Gibbs free energy of mixing per site becomes ΔGmix/kBT = ϕ1 ln ϕ1 + (ϕ2/r) ln ϕ2 + χ ϕ1ϕ2.

Phase separation occurs when the second derivative of ΔGmix with respect to ϕ2 becomes negative. The spinodal condition ∂²ΔGmix/∂ϕ2² = 0 yields χsp = ½(1/ϕ1 + 1/(rϕ2)). The binodal (coexistence) curve is obtained from the common‑tangent construction on ΔGmix(ϕ2). For high molecular weight (r ≫ 1) the critical point approaches ϕ2,c ≈ r⁻¹ᐟ² and χc ≈ ½ + r⁻¹ᐟ². The θ‑temperature is defined by χ(θ) = ½; at this temperature the polymer behaves ideally (second virial coefficient A₂ = 0) and the chain dimensions are unperturbed. Below θ the solvent is poor (χ > ½) and the polymer collapses; above θ the solvent is good (χ < ½) and the chain swells.

The theory predicts that increasing r narrows the two‑phase region and lowers the critical polymer concentration, a trend observed experimentally in polystyrene/cyclohexane and poly(methyl methacrylate)/acetone systems. Extensions include concentration‑dependent χ(ϕ), compressible lattice models, and the incorporation of specific interactions (hydrogen bonding) via association models. The Flory–Huggins framework also underpins the thermodynamics of polymer blends (χAB replaces χ) and block‑copolymer microphase separation, where the product χN governs the order‑disorder transition.

Worked Example: Determining χ from Cloud‑Point Data
A polystyrene (Mn = 2.0 × 10⁵ g mol⁻¹) solution in cyclohexane shows a cloud point at 35 °C for a polymer volume fraction ϕ₂ = 0.02. Assuming the critical composition approximates the cloud point for this high‑r polymer, estimate χ at 35 °C.
For a high‑r polymer the critical volume fraction ϕ₂,c ≈ r⁻¹ᐟ². The degree of polymerization r = Mn/M₀ where M₀ ≈ 104 g mol⁻¹ (styrene repeat unit). Thus r ≈ 2.0 × 10⁵ / 104 ≈ 1923. Then ϕ₂,c ≈ 1923⁻¹ᐟ² ≈ 0.0228, close to the observed 0.02, confirming the critical approximation. At the critical point χc = ½ + r⁻¹ᐟ² ≈ 0.5 + 0.0228 = 0.5228. Therefore χ(35 °C) ≈ 0.523.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question supplies cloud‑point temperature and polymer molecular weight, first compute r and the theoretical critical ϕ₂,c. If the given ϕ₂ matches ϕ₂,c within a factor of two, use the critical‑point expression χc = ½ + r⁻¹ᐟ². This avoids solving the full binodal equations and saves time.

Thermodynamics of Binding and Cooperativity in Biological Macromolecules

Ligand binding to a macromolecule with n identical sites is described by the binding polynomial Q = ∑i=0n Ki[L]ⁱ, where Ki are the macroscopic association constants. The fractional saturation Y = (1/n) ∂lnQ/∂ln[L]. For independent identical sites Ki = (n choose i) Kⁱ, yielding the hyperbolic Langmuir isotherm Y = K[L]/(1 + K[L]). Positive cooperativity makes the binding curve sigmoidal; the Hill equation Y = [L]ʰ/(Kdʰ + [L]ʰ) provides an empirical fit with Hill coefficient h > 1. The Hill plot (log[Y/(1 − Y)] vs log[L]) gives h as the slope at half‑saturation.

Two classic allosteric models rationalize cooperativity. The Monod–Wyman–Changeux (MWC) concerted model assumes the protein exists in two conformations, T (tense, low affinity) and R (relaxed, high affinity), with an allosteric constant L₀ = [T]₀/[R]₀. Ligand binds with affinities KT and KR (KR ≫ KT). The fractional saturation is Y = (α(1 + α)ⁿ⁻¹ + L₀ c α(1 + cα)ⁿ⁻¹)/((1 + α)ⁿ + L₀(1 + cα)ⁿ) where α = KR[L] and c = KT/KR. The Koshland–Némethy–Filmer (KNF) sequential model allows induced‑fit conformational changes at each binding step, giving microscopic constants that vary with occupancy. Both models predict that the free‑energy coupling between sites ΔΔG = −RT ln (Ki+1/Ki) quantifies cooperativity.

Experimental determination of binding thermodynamics uses isothermal titration calorimetry (ITC), which directly yields ΔH, Ka, and stoichiometry n in a single experiment. The van’t Hoff analysis of Ka(T) provides ΔH₍ᵥₕ₎; agreement between calorimetric and van’t Hoff enthalpies validates a two‑state binding model. For multisite proteins, global fitting of ITC data to the MWC or KNF equations extracts microscopic parameters. Cooperativity is also central to oxygen transport by hemoglobin (Hill coefficient ≈ 2.8), enzyme regulation (aspartate transcarbamoylase), and signal transduction (G‑protein coupled receptors). Understanding the thermodynamic linkage between ligand binding, conformational change, and function is a cornerstone of molecular biophysics.

Virial Equation of State and Corresponding States Principle for Real Gases

The compressibility factor Z = pVm/RT of a real gas can be expanded in powers of molar density ρ = 1/Vm (virial expansion in density) or pressure (virial expansion in pressure). The density form Z = 1 + B(T)ρ + C(T)ρ² + … defines the second virial coefficient B(T) = −(1/2)∫(e−u(r)/kBT − 1) 4πr² dr for pairwise additive potentials u(r). For a Lennard‑Jones 12‑6 potential B*(T*) = B/(b₀) where b₀ = 2πNAσ³/3 and T* = kBT/ε; tabulated reduced values allow rapid estimation. The Boyle temperature TB satisfies B(TB) = 0; at TB the gas behaves ideally over a wide pressure range.

The principle of corresponding states asserts that all fluids obey the same reduced equation of state when expressed in terms of reduced temperature Tr = T/Tc, reduced pressure pr = p/pc, and reduced volume Vr = V/Vc. For simple (spherical) molecules the two‑parameter corresponding‑states correlation works well; for polar or associating fluids a third parameter, the acentric factor ω = −log₁₀(prsat)Tr=0.7 − 1, extends the correlation (Pitzer’s three‑parameter method). Generalized compressibility charts plot Z versus pr for various Tr and ω, enabling property estimation for substances lacking accurate equations of state.

Higher virial coefficients C(T), D(T)… account for three‑body, four‑body interactions. For the hard‑sphere fluid the virial coefficients are known analytically: B = 2πσ³/3, C = 5π²σ⁶/18, D = 0.2869π³σ⁹… The convergence radius of the virial series is limited by the closest singularity (condensation). In practice, truncated virial equations (up to C) are reliable up to about 0.5 ρc. For engineering calculations the Lee–Kesler or Peng–Robinson cubic equations of state, which embed corresponding‑states ideas, are preferred. Nevertheless, the virial route provides a direct link between intermolecular potential and macroscopic PVT behavior, essential for developing force fields in molecular simulation.

Thermodynamic Analysis of Photochemical Reactions and Quantum Yield

A photochemical reaction absorbs photons of energy Eph = hν, promoting reactants to excited electronic states. The primary quantum yield Φprim is the number of primary events (e.g., bond cleavage, isomerization) per photon absorbed. The overall quantum yield Φ for product formation may differ due to secondary thermal steps, quenching, or chain reactions. The rate of product formation rP = Φ Iabs, where Iabs is the photon flux absorbed (einstein s⁻¹). Actinometry (e.g., ferrioxalate, potassium ferrioxalate) provides a calibrated Iabs for quantum‑yield determination.

The free‑energy change for a photochemical step can be expressed as ΔGphoto = ΔGthermal − NAhν, where ΔGthermal is the ground‑state reaction free energy. If ΔGphoto < 0 the photoreaction is thermodynamically allowed; however, kinetic barriers (intersystem crossing, conical intersections) control the actual Φ. The energy conversion efficiency η = (ΔGthermal × Φ)/(NAhν) quantifies how much photon energy is stored as chemical free energy. For the photosynthesis water‑splitting reaction (2 H₂O → 2 H₂ + O₂, ΔG° ≈ +474 kJ mol⁻¹) using 680 nm photons (176 kJ mol⁻¹ per photon) the theoretical maximum η is ≈ 30 % assuming Φ = 1 and four photons per O₂ evolved.

Photostationary states arise when forward and reverse photoreactions reach a dynamic equilibrium under constant irradiation. The composition satisfies Φfεf[A] = Φrεr[B] where ε are molar absorptivities at the irradiation wavelength. This principle underlies photochromic materials and optical data storage. In atmospheric chemistry, the quantum yield of NO₂ photolysis (NO₂ + hν → NO + O) determines ozone production rates; temperature‑dependent Φ values are incorporated into kinetic models. Accurate quantum‑yield measurement requires correction for inner‑filter effects, scattering, and secondary absorbance by products. The interplay of thermodynamics (ΔG), kinetics (rate constants of excited‑state decay), and photon flux governs the feasibility and efficiency of solar‑fuel and photodegradation processes.

  • Confusing the spinodal condition (∂²G/∂x² = 0) with the binodal (common‑tangent) when identifying metastable vs unstable regions.
  • Using the Flory–Huggins χ parameter as temperature‑independent; χ = A + B/T is the standard form for polymer solutions.
  • Assuming the Hill coefficient equals the number of binding sites; h ≤ n and reflects cooperativity, not stoichiometry.
  • Neglecting the acentric factor ω when applying corresponding‑states correlations to polar fluids; two‑parameter charts give large errors for water, alcohols, and refrigerants.
  • Treating the overall quantum yield as equal to the primary quantum yield in chain photochemical reactions; chain length can make Φ ≫ 1.
  • Applying the van’t Hoff equation to ITC data without checking for heat‑capacity changes; ΔCp ≠ 0 makes ln K vs 1/T non‑linear.
  • Using the ideal‑gas virial coefficient B(T) for dense gases beyond 0.5 ρc; higher‑order terms or a cubic EOS are required.

These advanced topics extend the thermodynamic toolkit to nucleation phenomena, polymer solution phase behavior, macromolecular binding cooperativity, real‑gas virial and corresponding‑states methods, and the thermodynamics of light‑driven reactions.

Thermodynamics of Electrolyte Solutions: Pitzer Equations and Activity Coefficients

The Debye–Hückel limiting law accurately predicts mean ionic activity coefficients only at very low ionic strengths (I < 0.01 mol kg⁻¹). For concentrated electrolyte solutions the extended Debye–Hückel equation adds a linear term in I, but deviations remain large because short‑range ion‑pair and ion‑solvent interactions are ignored. The Pitzer formalism, introduced in 1973, provides a virial‑type expansion of the excess Gibbs energy that remains reliable up to 6 mol kg⁻¹ for many 1:1 and 2:1 electrolytes. The excess molar Gibbs energy Gᴱ is written as a sum of a long‑range Debye–Hückel term and a short‑range virial series containing binary (β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, β⁽²⁾) and ternary (Cᵩ) interaction parameters that are functions of temperature and, for some models, pressure. For a single electrolyte MX the mean activity coefficient γ± follows from ln γ± = |z₊z₋|f(I) + m (2β⁽⁰⁾ + 2β⁽¹⁾ g(α₁√I) + 2β⁽²⁾ g(α₂√I)) + m² Cᵩ, where m is molality, f(I) is the Debye–Hückel function, and g(x) = 2(1 − (1 + x)e⁻ˣ)/x². The parameters β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, β⁽²⁾, Cᵩ are tabulated for hundreds of electrolytes and are regressed from osmotic coefficient and activity data. In mixed‑electrolyte systems the formalism adds cross‑interaction terms θᵢⱼ and ψᵢⱼₖ that account for cation–cation, anion–anion, and triple‑ion interactions. The Pitzer model is the standard for geochemical modelling (e.g., PHREEQC, Geochemist’s Workbench) and for designing industrial processes such as seawater desalination and nuclear waste brine chemistry. A common examination trap is to apply the Debye–Hückel limiting law to a 1 mol kg⁻¹ NaCl solution and report a γ± ≈ 0.66; the correct Pitzer value at 25 °C is ≈ 0.66 only by coincidence — at 3 mol kg⁻¹ the limiting law gives γ± > 1 while Pitzer yields ≈ 0.71.

Thermodynamics of Adsorption: Langmuir, BET, and Gibbs Isotherms

Adsorption equilibria are described by isotherms that relate the surface excess Γ (mol m⁻²) to the bulk concentration or pressure. The Langmuir model assumes a homogeneous surface with a fixed number of identical sites, monolayer coverage, and no lateral interactions. The fractional coverage θ = K p/(1 + K p) for gases (or θ = K c/(1 + K c) for solutes) leads to the linearised form p/θ = 1/K + p, where K is the equilibrium constant for adsorption. The BET (Brunauer–Emmett–Teller) equation extends Langmuir to multilayer adsorption by allowing molecules in the second and higher layers to behave like the bulk condensate. The BET form is p/[θ(p₀ − p)] = 1/(θₘC) + (C − 1)p/(θₘC p₀), where p₀ is the saturation vapour pressure, θₘ the monolayer capacity, and C the BET constant related to the heat of adsorption in the first layer. The Gibbs adsorption isotherm, derived from the fundamental equation dγ = −Σ Γᵢ dμᵢ, provides a model‑independent route to surface excess from surface tension measurements: Γ₂ = −(1/RT)(dγ/d ln a₂) for a binary solution. In practice, the Gibbs equation is used to determine Γ for surfactants below the critical micelle concentration, while Langmuir and BET are fitted to volumetric or gravimetric adsorption data. A frequent error is to apply the BET equation outside its valid relative pressure range (0.05 < p/p₀ < 0.35) and to ignore the requirement that C > 0 for physical consistency. Another trap is confusing the Gibbs surface excess (which can be negative for solvent) with the absolute adsorbed amount; the Gibbs dividing surface is a mathematical construct, not a physical layer.

Worked Example: Determining Monolayer Capacity from BET Data
Nitrogen adsorption at 77 K on a silica sample gives the following linear BET plot: p/[θ(p₀ − p)] versus p/p₀ yields a slope of 0.042 kPa⁻¹ and an intercept of 0.008 kPa⁻¹. Calculate the monolayer capacity θₘ (mmol g⁻¹) and the BET constant C.
From the BET linear form: slope = (C − 1)/(θₘC p₀) and intercept = 1/(θₘC p₀). Dividing slope by intercept gives (C − 1) = 0.042/0.008 = 5.25, so C = 6.25. Then θₘ = 1/(intercept × C × p₀). At 77 K, p₀(N₂) ≈ 101.3 kPa. θₘ = 1/(0.008 × 6.25 × 101.3) = 0.197 mmol g⁻¹. The monolayer capacity is 0.197 mmol g⁻¹ and C = 6.25.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the monolayer capacity from BET data, always check that the linear region lies within 0.05–0.35 p/p₀. If the given points fall outside, state that the BET model is not applicable and suggest the use of the Langmuir or DFT method instead. This nuance often separates full‑mark answers from partial credit.

Thermodynamics of Phase Transitions: Landau Theory and Critical Exponents

Landau theory provides a phenomenological description of continuous (second‑order) phase transitions by expanding the Gibbs free energy density G in powers of an order parameter η that vanishes in the high‑symmetry phase. For a ferromagnet η = M (magnetisation); for a binary liquid mixture η = Δx (composition difference). Near the critical temperature T_c the expansion reads G = G₀ + a(T − T_c)η² + bη⁴ + … with a > 0, b > 0. Minimisation yields η = 0 for T > T_c and η = ±√[a(T_c − T)/(2b)] for T < T_c, giving the mean‑field critical exponent β = 1/2. The susceptibility χ = (∂η/∂h)ₕ₌₀ diverges as |T − T_c|⁻¹ (γ = 1), the specific heat shows a finite jump (α = 0), and the correlation length ξ ∝ |T − T_c|⁻¹ᐟ² (ν = 1/2). These mean‑field exponents satisfy the Rushbrooke (α + 2β + γ = 2) and Widom (γ = β(δ − 1)) scaling relations with δ = 3. Real systems, however, exhibit non‑classical exponents due to critical fluctuations: for the three‑dimensional Ising universality class β ≈ 0.326, γ ≈ 1.237, ν ≈ 0.630, α ≈ 0.110. The Ginzburg criterion quantifies the temperature window where mean‑field theory fails: |T − T_c|/T_c < Gi, where Gi ∝ (k_B T_c)²/(ΔC ξ₀³) for fluids. In examinations, a typical question asks to identify the order of a transition from the behaviour of the order parameter or to apply the Clausius–Clapeyron equation to a first‑order line and contrast it with the Ehrenfest relations for second‑order transitions: (dP/dT) = Δα/Δκ_T = ΔC_p/(T V Δα). Remember that the Ehrenfest relations assume continuity of the first derivatives of G (volume, entropy) but discontinuity of second derivatives (compressibility, heat capacity, thermal expansion). A common trap is to treat the λ‑transition in helium‑4 as a first‑order transition because of the sharp heat‑capacity peak; it is actually a second‑order transition with a logarithmic divergence of C_p (α ≈ 0).

Thermodynamics of Non‑Ideal Gas Mixtures: Fugacity Coefficients and Mixing Rules

For a pure real gas the fugacity coefficient φ = f/p is obtained from the residual Gibbs energy: ln φ = ∫₀ᴾ (Z − 1) dP/P at constant T. In mixtures the fugacity coefficient of component i, φᵢ, depends on composition through the mixture compressibility factor Z_mix. Using a cubic equation of state (e.g., Peng–Robinson) the mixture parameters a_mix and b_mix are constructed from pure‑component parameters a_i, b_i and binary interaction parameters k_ij via the van der Waals one‑fluid mixing rules: a_mix = ΣᵢΣⱼ xᵢxⱼ √(aᵢaⱼ)(1 − k_ij), b_mix = Σᵢ xᵢ bᵢ. The fugacity coefficient of component i in the mixture is then ln φᵢ = (bᵢ/b_mix)(Z − 1) − ln(Z − B) + (A/(2√2 B))[2Σⱼ xⱼ aᵢⱼ/a_mix − bᵢ/b_mix] ln[(Z + (1 + √2)B)/(Z + (1 − √2)B)], where A = a_mix P/(R²T²), B = b_mix P/(RT), and aᵢⱼ = √(aᵢaⱼ)(1 − k_ij). The binary interaction parameter k_ij is often regressed from vapour‑liquid equilibrium data; for hydrocarbon pairs k_ij ≈ 0, but for polar/non‑polar pairs it can be 0.1–0.2. An alternative is the Wong–Sandler mixing rule, which enforces the correct low‑pressure limit by combining an excess‑Gibbs‑energy model (e.g., NRTL) with the EOS. In high‑pressure phase‑equilibrium calculations (e.g., CO₂ + hydrocarbon systems for enhanced oil recovery) the choice of mixing rule strongly affects predicted bubble‑point pressures. A frequent examination error is to use the pure‑component fugacity coefficient φ_i^pure in place of the mixture φᵢ when evaluating the equilibrium condition yᵢ φᵢ^V P = xᵢ φᵢ^L P. The mixture φᵢ accounts for cross‑interactions and can differ from φ_i^pure by orders of magnitude at high pressures.

Thermodynamics of Chemical Reaction Networks: Stoichiometric Constraints and Reaction Affinity

In complex reaction systems (metabolic pathways, combustion mechanisms, catalytic cycles) the second law is applied through the concept of reaction affinity A_r = −Σᵢ νᵢᵣ μᵢ, where νᵢᵣ are stoichiometric coefficients (negative for reactants, positive for products) of reaction r. For a network of R reactions the vector of affinities A = −Nᵀ μ, with N the stoichiometric matrix (species × reactions). At equilibrium all affinities vanish, A_r = 0, which yields the familiar equilibrium constants K_r = exp(−Δ_rG°/RT). Away from equilibrium the entropy production rate σ = (1/T) Σᵣ J_r A_r ≥ 0, where J_r are reaction rates (extent per unit time). The stoichiometric matrix defines conserved moieties (left null vectors of N) that correspond to conserved quantities such as total mass, elemental atoms, or moiety pools (e.g., ATP/ADP/AMP). Flux balance analysis (FBA) uses the steady‑state condition N J = 0 together with thermodynamic feasibility constraints A_r J_r ≥ 0 to predict feasible flux distributions without kinetic parameters. In examinations, a typical problem provides a small network (e.g., glycolysis segment) and asks to identify the independent reactions, compute the rank of N, and write the affinity expressions. A common trap is to treat all reactions as independent when the stoichiometric matrix is rank‑deficient; the number of independent affinities equals rank(N). Another error is to assume that a negative Δ_rG° guarantees a forward flux; the actual direction depends on the instantaneous concentrations through A_r = −Δ_rG° + RT ln Q_r, where Q_r is the reaction quotient. The principle of detailed balance further requires that at equilibrium each elementary step satisfies forward and reverse rates equality, imposing constraints on the kinetic constants (Wegscheider conditions).

  • Applying the Debye–Hückel limiting law to ionic strengths above 0.01 mol kg⁻¹ and reporting activity coefficients that are physically impossible (γ > 1 for strong electrolytes).
  • Using the BET equation outside the 0.05–0.35 p/p₀ range or ignoring the requirement C > 0, leading to negative monolayer capacities.
  • Confusing the Gibbs surface excess (which can be negative) with the absolute adsorbed amount and forgetting the arbitrary location of the Gibbs dividing surface.
  • Assuming mean‑field critical exponents (β = ½, γ = 1) apply to real fluids near the critical point; real systems belong to the Ising universality class with non‑classical exponents.
  • Replacing mixture fugacity coefficients φᵢ with pure‑component φ_i^pure in vapour‑liquid equilibrium calculations for high‑pressure non‑ideal mixtures.
  • Treating all reactions in a network as thermodynamically independent without checking the rank of the stoichiometric matrix, resulting in over‑specified affinity equations.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of Pitzer parameters β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, Cᵩ when extrapolating to temperatures far from the regression range.

Thermodynamics of Non‑Ideal Solution Models: UNIFAC and UNIQUAC

Accurate prediction of activity coefficients in multicomponent liquid mixtures is essential for distillation, extraction, and reactor design. The UNIQUAC (Universal Quasi‑Chemical) model combines a combinatorial term derived from lattice‑fluid theory with a residual term based on surface‑area‑weighted interaction energies. The combinatorial part accounts for molecular size and shape differences through volume (r) and surface‑area (q) parameters, while the residual part uses binary interaction parameters τij = exp(−aij/RT) where aij are energy parameters regressed from vapour‑liquid equilibrium (VLE) data. UNIFAC (UNIQUAC Functional‑group Activity Coefficients) extends this by decomposing molecules into functional groups (CH3, CH2, OH, COOH, etc.) and using group‑interaction parameters amn that are transferable across many systems. The group‑contribution approach enables prediction for mixtures where no experimental VLE data exist. In examinations, a typical problem supplies a ternary mixture (e.g., ethanol‑water‑benzene) and asks to compute γi at a given composition using UNIFAC group‑volume (Rk) and group‑surface‑area (Qk) values from the standard tables (e.g., Dortmund Data Bank). A frequent trap is to neglect the temperature dependence of the group interaction parameters amn(T) = amn0 + amn1·T + amn2·T2, which becomes significant above 350 K. Another error is to apply UNIFAC to strongly associating systems (e.g., water‑glycerol) without the modified UNIFAC‑Dortmund version that includes a hydrogen‑bonding term. The UNIQUAC combinatorial expression ln γiC = ln(φi/xi) + (z/2) qi ln(θi/φi) + li − (φi/xi) Σj xj lj (with φi = xiri/Σxjrj, θi = xiqi/Σxjqj, li = (z/2)(ri−qi)−(ri−1), z = 10) must be evaluated with consistent r and q values; mixing r from one source and q from another yields non‑physical γ > 10 for dilute components.

Thermodynamics of Micellization: Critical Micelle Concentration and Aggregation Thermodynamics

Surfactant self‑assembly into micelles is driven by the hydrophobic effect and governed by a balance between the free energy of transferring hydrocarbon tails from water into a non‑polar core and the penalty of creating a curved interface. The standard free energy of micellization per mole of surfactant is ΔGmic° = RT ln (CMC) (for non‑ionic surfactants) or ΔGmic° = (2−α)RT ln (CMC) for ionic surfactants, where α is the degree of counter‑ion binding (typically 0.2–0.4 for sodium alkyl sulfates). The enthalpy of micellization ΔHmic can be measured directly by isothermal titration calorimetry (ITC); it is often small and changes sign near the Krafft temperature. The entropy contribution ΔSmic = (ΔHmic−ΔGmic)/T is usually positive because water molecules released from ordered hydration shells around the tails gain translational freedom. The aggregation number Nagg (typically 50–100 for spherical micelles) relates to the micelle molar mass Mmic = Nagg Msurf and can be obtained from static light scattering (Rayleigh ratio) or small‑angle neutron scattering (SANS). The critical micelle concentration (CMC) decreases with increasing alkyl chain length (≈ factor 2 per CH2 group) and increases with temperature for non‑ionics (due to dehydration of ethylene‑oxide head groups) but shows a minimum for ionics. In exam questions, a common data set provides CMC at two temperatures (e.g., 0.008 M at 298 K and 0.006 M at 308 K for SDS) and asks to calculate ΔHmic using the van’t Hoff relation d ln CMC/d(1/T) = −ΔHmic/R. Students often forget the (2−α) factor for ionic surfactants, leading to a systematic underestimation of ΔHmic by ~30 %. Another trap is to treat the CMC as a sharp phase transition; in reality the monomer‑micelle equilibrium is broad, and the “CMC” defined by surface‑tension break point differs from that obtained by conductivity or fluorescence by up to 15 %.

Worked Example: van’t Hoff Analysis of SDS Micellization
The critical micelle concentration of sodium dodecyl sulfate (SDS) is measured as 8.2 × 10−3 mol L−1 at 298 K and 6.5 × 10−3 mol L−1 at 308 K. The degree of counter‑ion binding α = 0.30. Calculate the standard enthalpy of micellization ΔHmic° (in kJ mol−1).
For ionic surfactants ΔGmic° = (2−α)RT ln CMC. The van’t Hoff equation gives d ln CMC/d(1/T) = −ΔHmic°/R. Using the two points: ln(CMC2/CMC1) = ln(6.5/8.2) = −0.233. Δ(1/T) = 1/308 − 1/298 = −1.09 × 10−4 K−1. Slope = (−0.233)/(−1.09 × 10−4) = 2138 K. ΔHmic° = −R × slope = −8.314 J mol−1K−1 × 2138 K = −17.8 kJ mol−1. The negative sign indicates exothermic micellization at these temperatures.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem gives CMC at two temperatures, always check whether the surfactant is ionic or non‑ionic. Write the correct ΔG‑CMC relation first, then apply the van’t Hoff slope. Keep α explicit; if not given, assume α ≈ 0.3 for common alkyl sulfates and state the assumption.

Thermodynamics of Biomolecular Condensates: Liquid–Liquid Phase Separation in Cells

Membraneless organelles (nucleoli, stress granules, P‑bodies) form via liquid–liquid phase separation (LLPS) of multivalent proteins and nucleic acids. The thermodynamic framework treats the system as a binary (or multicomponent) mixture undergoing a demixing transition described by a Flory–Huggins‑type free energy density f(φ) = (φ/N) ln φ + (1−φ) ln(1−φ) + χ φ(1−φ), where φ is the volume fraction of the scaffold protein, N its degree of polymerization (number of interaction domains), and χ the effective interaction parameter capturing sticker‑spacer architecture, charge patterning, and solvent quality. The spinodal curve is given by ∂2f/∂φ2 = 0 → 1/(Nφ) + 1/(1−φ) − 2χ = 0, and the binodal (coexistence curve) by equality of chemical potentials and osmotic pressures. For typical intrinsically disordered regions (IDRs) with N ≈ 10–100 and χ ≈ 0.5–1.5, the critical concentration φc ≈ 1/√N lies in the 1–10 µM range, matching cellular concentrations. Temperature dependence enters through χ(T) = A + B/T; many condensates exhibit upper critical solution temperature (UCST) behaviour (phase separation on cooling) because hydrophobic stickers become more associative at lower T, while others show lower critical solution temperature (LCST) due to temperature‑dependent hydration of polar spacers. Salt modulates χ via Debye‑Hückel screening of electrostatic stickers; increasing ionic strength often suppresses LLPS for charge‑driven condensates but can enhance it for hydrophobic‑driven ones by salting‑out. In examinations, a typical question provides a simplified two‑component phase diagram (φ vs T) and asks to identify the critical point, tie‑line construction, and the effect of a mutation that reduces sticker valency (decreasing N) on the binodal. A common mistake is to treat the scaffold as a simple polymer with N equal to the number of amino acids rather than the number of interaction motifs; this overestimates φc by an order of magnitude. Another trap is to ignore the contribution of RNA as a multivalent ligand that effectively increases the valency and shifts the binodal to lower protein concentrations.

Thermodynamics of Electrochemical Energy Storage: Batteries and Supercapacitors

The maximum electrical work obtainable from a galvanic cell is ΔG = −nF Ecell, where n is the number of electrons transferred per formula unit and F = 96485 C mol−1. For a lithium‑ion intercalation cathode (e.g., LiCoO2) the open‑circuit voltage (OCV) varies with lithium stoichiometry x in LixCoO2 according to the Nernst equation E = E° − (RT/F) ln aLi, where aLi is the activity of lithium in the host lattice. The activity is related to the chemical potential μLi = μLi° + RT ln aLi. In solid‑solution electrodes the OCV curve shows a smooth slope; in two‑phase regions (e.g., LiFePO4/FePO4) the voltage is flat because aLi is fixed by the coexistence of two phases with constant chemical potential. The theoretical specific capacity Cth = nF/M (M = molar mass of active material) for LiCoO2 (M ≈ 97.9 g mol−1, n = 1) is 274 mAh g−1. Energy density (Wh kg−1) = Cth × average voltage. For supercapacitors, energy is stored electrostatically in the electric double layer (EDL) or via fast surface redox (pseudocapacitance). The differential capacitance Cdl = dQ/dV follows the Gouy‑Chapman‑Stern model: 1/Cdl = 1/CHelmholtz + 1/CGouy‑Chapman, with CGouy‑Chapman = εε0κ cosh(zFV/2RT) where κ = √(2z2F2c/εε0RT). The maximum energy per unit electrode mass is E = ½ Csp V2, where Csp is the specific capacitance (F g−1) and V the operating window limited by electrolyte decomposition. In exam problems, a typical data set gives the OCV profile of a LixMn2O4 cathode at 298 K and asks to compute the entropy change ΔS = nF (dE/dT) from the temperature coefficient of the voltage (often measured by galvanostatic intermittent titration technique, GITT). A frequent error is to use the full cell voltage instead of the half‑cell potential versus Li/Li+ when calculating ΔS for a single electrode. Another trap is to assume the double‑layer capacitance is constant; in reality Cdl varies with potential and ion concentration, and the simple ½ CV2 formula overestimates energy if the voltage window includes regions where C drops sharply.

Thermodynamics of Quantum Systems: Fluctuation Theorems and Quantum Heat Engines

Classical stochastic thermodynamics is extended to the quantum regime by defining work and heat through two‑point measurement protocols or continuous monitoring of system observables. The quantum Jarzynski equality ⟨e−βW⟩ = e−βΔF holds for a system initially in a thermal state ρ = e−βH(0)/Z(0) and driven by a time‑dependent Hamiltonian H(t). The average exponentiated work is evaluated over projective energy measurements at t = 0 and t = τ. The Crooks fluctuation theorem PF(W)/PR(−W) = eβ(W−ΔF) relates forward and reverse work distributions. For a quantum Otto engine operating between hot (Th) and cold (Tc) reservoirs with a working medium of a single harmonic oscillator (frequency ω), the efficiency at maximum power is ηMP = 1 − √(Tc/Th) (Curzon‑Ahlborn) only in the high‑temperature limit (kBT ≫ ħω). In the quantum regime (kBT ≲ ħω) the efficiency is reduced because the occupation numbers become discrete and the adiabatic strokes must be performed slowly enough to avoid non‑adiabatic excitations (quantum friction). The power output P = W/τcycle is maximized by optimizing the stroke durations; the optimal cycle time scales as τopt ∝ ħ/(kBT). Experimental realizations use trapped ions (e.g., 40Ca+) where the oscillator frequency is tuned by the trap potential, and the reservoirs are engineered by laser cooling and heating. In examinations, a typical question provides the Hamiltonian H(t) = ħω(t)(a†a + ½) with a linear frequency ramp ω(t) = ωi + (ωf−ωi)t/τ and asks to compute the average work ⟨W⟩ = ħ(ωf−ωi)(n̄ + ½) where n̄ = 1/(eβħωi−1). Students often forget the zero‑point energy contribution (½ħω) which cancels only if the initial and final frequencies are equal; otherwise it contributes to ΔF and must be included in the Jarzynski average. Another common error is to apply the classical Carnot efficiency ηC = 1 − Tc/Th to a quantum engine without checking the condition kBT ≫ ħω; the correct quantum Carnot bound remains ηC but the achievable efficiency at finite power is lower due to quantum coherence and measurement back‑action.

  • Using UNIFAC group parameters from an outdated database (pre‑2000) for systems containing fluorinated or silicon‑based groups, leading to γ errors > 50 %.
  • Assuming the CMC is a true thermodynamic critical point and applying scaling laws (e.g., γ ∝ |T−Tc|−μ) to surfactant micellization.
  • Neglecting the RNA‑mediated increase in effective valency when modelling biomolecular condensates, resulting in predicted saturation concentrations an order of magnitude too high.
  • Applying the full‑cell voltage to compute single‑electrode entropy changes in Li‑ion batteries, ignoring the reference electrode contribution.
  • Treating the double‑layer capacitance as voltage‑independent when estimating supercapacitor energy density, especially near the electrolyte decomposition limits.
  • Omitting the zero‑point energy term in quantum work calculations for frequency‑changing protocols, causing systematic deviation from the Jarzynski equality.
  • Using the high‑temperature Curzon‑Ahlborn efficiency for a quantum Otto engine operating at kBT ≈ ħω, overestimating the maximum power efficiency by 20–30 %.

Thermodynamics of Supercritical Fluids: Properties and Applications

A supercritical fluid exists above its critical temperature Tc and critical pressure Pc, where distinct liquid and gas phases merge into a single homogeneous phase. The critical point is defined by the conditions (∂P/∂V)T = 0 and (∂²P/∂V²)T = 0 on the equation of state. For carbon dioxide, Tc = 304.1 K and Pc = 7.38 MPa. Near the critical point, the isothermal compressibility κT = –(1/V)(∂V/∂P)T diverges, causing large density fluctuations (critical opalescence). The molar enthalpy H and entropy S vary sharply with pressure at constant temperature, which is exploited in supercritical extraction: a small pressure drop near Pc produces a large change in solvating power because the fluid density ρ changes from liquid‑like to gas‑like. The residual Gibbs energy GR = G – Gig quantifies non‑ideality; for a supercritical fluid it is obtained by integrating the compressibility factor Z = PV/RT from zero pressure to the system pressure: GR/RT = ∫₀ᴾ (Z–1) dP/P. In exam problems, the Peng–Robinson or Soave–Redlich–Kwong cubic equations of state are frequently supplied; students must compute fugacity coefficients φ = exp[(GR/RT)] and then the solubility of a solid solute via ln(x₂φ₂) = –ΔHfus/R (1/T – 1/Tfus). A common trap is to treat the supercritical fluid as an ideal gas when calculating the work of compression; the correct reversible work is W = ∫ V dP using the real‑fluid V(P,T) from the chosen EOS.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquids: Structure, Solvation, and Phase Behavior

Ionic liquids (ILs) are salts with melting points below 373 K, composed of bulky asymmetric cations (e.g., 1‑alkyl‑3‑methylimidazolium) and weakly coordinating anions (e.g., [BF₄]⁻, [PF₆]⁻, [NTf₂]⁻). Their negligible vapour pressure makes the chemical potential of the vapour phase effectively infinite, so the liquid phase is the reference state. The standard molar Gibbs energy of formation ΔfG° of an IL is obtained from lattice‑energy calculations (Born–Haber cycle) combined with solvation free energies of the ions. The enthalpy of vaporization ΔvapH is large (150–250 kJ mol⁻¹) because breaking the Coulombic network requires substantial energy; however, the entropy of vaporization ΔvapS is also large, reflecting the high degree of order in the liquid. The temperature dependence of the viscosity η follows the Vogel–Fulcher–Tammann (VFT) equation η = η₀ exp[B/(T–T₀)], where T₀ is the ideal glass‑transition temperature. In binary mixtures with molecular solvents, the excess molar volume VE and excess enthalpy HE are often negative due to strong ion‑dipole interactions and packing effects. The COSMO‑RS model predicts activity coefficients γᵢ from σ‑profiles of the ions; exam questions may provide σ‑moments and ask for the liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) tie‑line using the equality of component fugacities x₁γ₁ = x₂γ₂. A frequent error is to assume Raoult’s law holds for the molecular component; the correct relation uses the IL as a non‑volatile solvent with γ₁ → ∞ for the IL itself, so the solvent activity is a₁ = x₁γ₁ and the IL activity is taken as unity.

Worked Example: Fugacity Coefficient of Supercritical CO₂
At 313 K and 10 MPa, the compressibility factor of CO₂ from the Peng–Robinson EOS is Z = 0.78. Compute the fugacity coefficient φ and the fugacity f (in MPa).
The fugacity coefficient is φ = exp[∫₀ᴾ (Z–1) dP/P]. For a single‑point estimate at moderate pressure, use the approximation φ ≈ exp[(Z–1) ln(P/P°)] with P° = 1 MPa. Here (Z–1) = –0.22, ln(10) = 2.3026, so ln φ = –0.22 × 2.3026 = –0.5066 → φ = 0.603. Fugacity f = φ P = 0.603 × 10 MPa = 6.03 MPa. (If the full integral is required, the same result is obtained because Z is nearly constant over the range.)
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem gives a cubic EOS and asks for fugacity, first check whether the pressure is low enough for the virial approximation (B P/RT). If not, use the analytical expression for φ from the EOS (e.g., ln φ = Z–1 – ln(Z–B) – A/(2√2 B) ln[(Z+(1+√2)B)/(Z+(1–√2)B)] for Peng–Robinson). Memorise the A and B parameter definitions: A = aαP/(R²T²), B = bP/(RT). This saves derivation time and avoids sign errors.

Thermodynamics of Metal–Organic Frameworks: Adsorption Isotherms and Flexibility

Metal–organic frameworks (MOFs) are crystalline porous solids built from metal nodes linked by organic ligands. Their adsorption thermodynamics is governed by the Gibbs free energy change ΔGads = ΔHads – TΔSads. The isosteric heat of adsorption qst is obtained from the Clausius–Clapeyron relation applied to isotherms at two temperatures: qst = –R (∂ln P/∂(1/T))n. For rigid MOFs, the Langmuir model n = nmax bP/(1+bP) with b = b₀ exp(–ΔHads/RT) fits low‑pressure data; at higher loadings, the BET or Dubinin–Astakhov equations account for multilayer filling and pore‑size distribution. Flexible MOFs (e.g., MIL‑53) exhibit a “breathing” transition: the framework contracts or expands at a gate pressure Pgate, producing a step in the isotherm. The transition is a first‑order phase change with a coexistence region where the chemical potential of the guest equals that of the host in both narrow‑pore (np) and large‑pore (lp) forms: μguest(P,T) = μnp = μlp. The free‑energy difference between the two host structures is ΔGhost = Glp – Gnp; adsorption stabilises the lp form when ΔGhost + nΔμads < 0. In exam questions, the gate pressure is often derived from the equality of the osmotic potentials: Πnp(P) = Πlp(P). A typical trap is to treat the step as a simple Langmuir saturation and ignore the hysteresis between adsorption and desorption branches; the correct analysis uses the Maxwell construction on the Π–V curve to locate the true equilibrium pressure.

Thermodynamics of Fuel Cells: Gibbs Energy, Overpotentials, and Efficiency Limits

A fuel cell converts the chemical free energy of a fuel (e.g., H₂) directly into electrical work. The maximum reversible cell voltage is given by the Nernst equation E = –ΔG/(nF) where ΔG = ΔG° + RT ln Q and Q = (aH₂O)/(aH₂ aO₂¹ᐟ²). At standard conditions (298 K, 1 bar), ΔG° = –237.13 kJ mol⁻¹ for liquid water, yielding E° = 1.229 V. The higher heating value (HHV) corresponds to ΔH° = –285.83 kJ mol⁻¹; the theoretical maximum efficiency based on enthalpy is ηmax = ΔG°/ΔH° = 0.83 (83 %). Real cells suffer three main overpotentials: activation (ηact), ohmic (ηohm = iRΩ), and concentration (ηconc). The Butler–Volmer equation describes activation loss: i = i₀ [exp(αₐ nF ηact/RT) – exp(–α_c nF ηact/RT)]. At high current density, the Tafel approximation ηact = (RT/α nF) ln(i/i₀) is used. The cell voltage under load is V = E – ηact – ηohm – ηconc. The practical efficiency is η = V/(ΔH°/nF). For a solid‑oxide fuel cell (SOFC) operating at 1073 K, the Nernst voltage rises because ΔG becomes less negative, but the ionic conductivity of the electrolyte (YSZ) follows an Arrhenius law σ = σ₀ exp(–Eₐ/RT). Exam problems often give polarization curves (V vs i) and ask to extract i₀, RΩ, and the limiting current iL from the concentration overpotential ηconc = (RT/nF) ln(1 – i/iL). A common mistake is to use the HHV efficiency denominator when the product water is vapour; then ΔH° = –241.8 kJ mol⁻¹ and the theoretical efficiency becomes 94 %.

Thermodynamics of Glass Transition: Configurational Entropy and the Kauzmann Paradox

The glass transition is not a true thermodynamic phase transition but a kinetic freezing of configurational degrees of freedom. Upon cooling a liquid at a constant rate, the configurational entropy Sconf decreases as the system explores fewer distinct amorphous packings. The Adam–Gibbs relation links the structural relaxation time τ to Sconf: τ = τ₀ exp[C/(T Sconf)]. Extrapolating the liquid entropy below the experimental glass‑transition temperature Tg leads to the Kauzmann temperature TK where Sconf would equal the crystal entropy, implying an “entropy crisis”. The Vogel–Fulcher–Tammann (VFT) equation τ = τ₀ exp[D T₀/(T–T₀)] with T₀ ≈ TK captures the divergence. The fragility index m = d log₁₀ τ / d(Tg/T) |T=Tg classifies glass‑formers: strong (m ≈ 20, e.g., SiO₂) follow Arrhenius behaviour, fragile (m > 100, e.g., o‑terphenyl) follow VFT. In differential scanning calorimetry (DSC), the heat‑capacity jump ΔCp = Cp,liquid – Cp,glass at Tg is used to estimate Sconf(T) = ∫TKᵀ ΔCp/T' dT'. Exam questions may provide ΔCp and Tg and ask for TK assuming Sconf(TK) = 0. A frequent trap is to confuse the kinetic Tg (which depends on cooling rate) with the thermodynamic TK; the correct answer states that Tg shifts to higher temperature with faster cooling, while TK is a material constant.

  • Assuming supercritical CO₂ behaves ideally when calculating compression work, ignoring the real‑fluid compressibility factor.
  • Applying Raoult’s law to the molecular solvent in an ionic‑liquid mixture instead of using activity coefficients from COSMO‑RS or experimental LLE data.
  • Treating the breathing step in a flexible MOF isotherm as a simple Langmuir saturation and neglecting hysteresis and the Maxwell construction.
  • Using the higher heating value (liquid water) as the efficiency denominator for a fuel cell that produces water vapour, overstating the theoretical efficiency.
  • Confusing the kinetic glass‑transition temperature Tg (cooling‑rate dependent) with the thermodynamic Kauzmann temperature TK (material constant).
  • Omitting the zero‑point energy contribution when evaluating the quantum Jarzynski equality for a frequency‑changing protocol, leading to systematic deviation from the predicted free‑energy difference.
  • Assuming the double‑layer capacitance of a supercapacitor is voltage‑independent across the entire operating window, which overestimates stored energy near electrolyte decomposition limits.

Thermodynamics of Protein Folding and Stability

Protein folding is governed by the balance between enthalpic gains from native contacts and the entropic cost of restricting conformational freedom. The standard Gibbs free energy of folding ΔG°_fold = ΔH°_fold – TΔS°_fold is typically in the range –20 to –60 kJ mol⁻¹ for small globular proteins at 298 K. The enthalpy term arises mainly from hydrogen bonds, van der Waals packing, and electrostatic interactions; the entropy term includes the loss of chain conformational entropy (≈ –1.5 kJ mol⁻¹ per residue) and the gain in solvent entropy due to burial of hydrophobic side chains (the hydrophobic effect). The temperature dependence of ΔG°_fold is described by the Gibbs–Helmholtz relation ΔG°_fold(T) = ΔH°_fold(T_ref) – TΔS°_fold(T_ref) + ΔC_p[(T – T_ref) – T ln(T/T_ref)], where ΔC_p is the heat‑capacity change upon folding, typically positive (0.5–2.5 kJ mol⁻¹ K⁻¹) because non‑polar surface area is removed from water. A positive ΔC_p leads to cold denaturation at low temperature and heat denaturation at high temperature, giving a stability curve that is roughly parabolic with a maximum near physiological temperature.

Two‑state versus multi‑state folding

Many small proteins exhibit apparent two‑state behaviour: only native (N) and unfolded (U) ensembles are significantly populated, so the equilibrium constant K = [U]/[N] = exp(–ΔG°_fold/RT).
